3 t Where we are G History teaches us that G there are inherent delays with any transformative G technology.
e Solow paradox, coined by economist Robert Solow in 1987, G claimed that while you could see computers everywhere, their inuence was G not then evident in productivity statistics.
is paradox was resolved in the H 1990s when large sectors of the economy, such as retail and wholesale, nally H adopted technologies such as client-server architectures and ERP (enterprise H resource planning) systems.
ese innovations helped revamp business processes, H particularly in supply chain and distribution center eciency, leading to noticeable H gains in productivity.
H Today seems to mirror the patterns of the past.

is H discrepancy exists primarily because large sectors of our economy have yet to H integrate AI into their businesses.

a AI Concepts ` e term articial intelligence has ` been used in computer science since the 1950s, ` with the Dartmouth conference in 1956 usually cited as its ocial birthplace.
` Since then, the eld of AI has been through a series of cycles of strong support a and investment versus periods of criticism and reduction of investment, referred a to as AI winters.
ese cycles throughout the 70s, 80s, and 90s had a material a impact on consistent progress.
a is situation fundamentally changed with the advent of big data and cloud a computing.
e aordability and power of data storage and CPU time increased a signicantly, making it possible to train models more eectively.
As a result, AI a models that were once theoretical and unattainable due to resource constraints a became practical and ecient.
a In the last ve years, the largest technology organizations in the world have a tackled and started to solve problems that were completely intractable only b ten years before.
We are at the start of a period of high investment and rapid b progress that is bringing new enthusiasm among a larger population.
However, b the outcome of this period remains uncertain.
b AI is a broad denition for a range of soware applications.
As a branch of b computer science, AI can be thought of as a smart computer that can do tasks b usually done by people.
It understands languages, recognizes patterns, solves b problems, makes decisions, and learns from experience.
It uses vast amounts b of data and follows instructions called algorithms to automate tasks.
b Comparing AI to traditional soware applications, for instance, if we want b soware to detect dogs in a picture, writing conventional soware becomes c complex and costly.
AI is more suitable for this task.
e process involves feeding c an AI system with thousands of pictures, some containing dogs and others c not.
e AI learns from these examples and develops an internal conguration c that can detect if a random image contains a dog.
is example illustrates how c AI is suitable for solving complex tasks that are near impossible to tackle with 9 c traditional soware.
is is a fundamental shi from traditional programming c which is designed to produce deterministic c results based on a set of inputs, as c AI learns from the data and makes predictions or decisions without having c to be explicitly programmed to perform the task.
@ Types of AI d At a high level, AI can be d categorized into two main types: narrow AI and d general AI.
D E F Narrow AI, also known as weak AI, is designed to perform a specic task, such G as voice recognition or image analysis.
ese solutions operate under a limited H set of constraints within a limited context and are only intelligent within I their specic domain.
Examples are frequently found in nance, for example, P scaling commercial contract reviews, augmenting collection prioritization, Q forecasting, streamlining invoice approvals, and anomaly detection.
e R examples given in the interviews in this book provide numerous use case S demonstrations of Narrow AI.
T U V General AI, also known as strong AI and Articial General Intelligence W (AGI), refers to systems that possess the ability to perform any intellectual X task that a human can do.
ey can understand, learn, adapt, and implement Y knowledge in dierent domains.
While this type of AI is not currently available, ` prominent AI researchers forecast that the rst AGI will be developed within a the next decade.
Nevertheless, it is the core of many philosophical debates b as while the advancement of AI may be inevitable, its ultimate destination c is not clear.
AIs function is complex and uncertain.
Some Large Language d Models (LLMs) have demonstrated advanced capabilities beyond narrow AI; e however, there are still substantial dierences between f these AI systems and g human cognitive systems.
10 f AI encompasses various interconnected areas: i p q Machine Learning (ML) is a subset r of AI.
It is the foundation that includes the s algorithms and statistical models that underpin AI.
It refers to the approaches t associated with getting computers to learn and act like humans, improving u their learning over time in an autonomous fashion, by feeding them data and v information in the form of observations and real-world interactions.
w x y Neural Networks (NNs) are a subset of machine learning, modeled aer the human brains architecture and designed to emulate its processing style.
ese networks consist of interconnected nodes, analogous to the brains neurons, organized in layers.
Data enters through the input layer, is processed through one or several layers where computation and transformation occur, and culminates in an output layer that delivers the nal results.
Each layers nodes are linked with weights numerical parameters that are adjusted during training enabling the network to discern complex patterns and learn from data.
Deep Learning (DL) is an advanced subset of NNs characterized by its use of several hidden layers that add complexity and the ability to perform complex tasks.
Deep learning approaches excel at processing and learning from extensive datasets and are instrumental in fueling many of the latest innovations in AI.
ese include generative AI models, computer vision, and self-driving soware.
e advantage here is the ability to handle and learn from very large datasets, allowing it to recognize complex patterns and make intelligent decisions.
e drawback is the almost complete absence of explainability.
For example, it does not explain why a dog is not recognized in a certain picture when there clearly is a dog present, or why the AI has denied credit to a specic person.
Generative AI (GenAI) is a subset of deep learning that generates multi-modal content such as text, videos, and images with user interactions.
It is trained on vast datasets to detect patterns and creates outputs without instruction.
Unlike databases, these models dont query and return 100% accurate historical information.
Instead, they mimic inputs and provide new content that is likely to be correct based on probabilistic techniques.
11 AI training techniques q Training a custom q model frequently involves the use of multiple techniques, q as shown in the following diagram.
q Figure 2.
1 A variety of AI training techniques r Some common techniques include: !
" # Supervised Learning, a traditional $ approach in machine learning where a % model is trained on a labeled dataset.
In this context, labeled means that & the dataset contains the answers to the questions we want the model to be ' able to answer.
( For example, if we want to train an ML model to predict customer churn, ) we can feed the model with historical data about our customers, including 0 age, gender, geographical location, purchasing behavior, and product usage.
1 Crucially, we augment this dataset with the churn data for each customer.
2 Churn, in this context, refers to when a customer stops using a product or 3 service.
Once trained, the model will be able to forecast the date when a 4 particular customer is likely to churn based on the available data for that 5 unique customer.
s 12 7 8 9 Reinforcement Learning (RL) addresses @ a key limitation of supervised and A unsupervised learning: their inability to discover novel mechanisms beyond the B relationships in the data.
Reinforcement Learning algorithms such as deep-Q C learning, or actor-critic, enhance eectiveness by allowing the exploration of D strategies not represented in the available data.
is approach is akin to A/B E testing F on steroids, where millions of dierent scenarios are automatically G generated, and the feedback loop is integral to the learning process.
H For example, the machine might experiment with varying discount levels to I identify the most eective discount at each stage of the customer journey.
P e RL system might issue random discounts to thousands of customers Q and then measure the churn rate.
is process renes the discount strategy R to optimal levels based on the knowledge of what works or not for specic S customer groups.
For example, the system might learn that customers with T children value a free month in August, while auent customers in rural U areas respond well to bundle discounts.
In this way, reinforcement learning V enables the discovery of eective, tailored strategies that were not initially W apparent in the data.
X Y ` Unsupervised Learning refers to an a ML approach where models are trained b on unlabeled data.
Unlike supervised learning, where we have explicit labels c for our data, unsupervised learning involves discovering underlying structures d without predened categories.
In this scenario, there arent specic targets to e predict, but we aim to uncover meaningful patterns, similarities, or f clusters g in the data.
h For example, by feeding customer data into the ML solution, we might discover i that customers without children exhibit similar behavior across dierent p geographical locations and income levels.
Armed with this insight, specic q marketing r campaigns can be developed to target this particular customer s segment eectively.
13 t AIs limitations w e current limitations of w AI systems include hallucinations, bias, w explainability, and scalability: x y Hallucinations: AI hallucinations refer to inaccurate or misleading results generated by AI models.
ese errors can arise due to various factors, including limited training data, faulty assumptions made by the model, or biases present in the training data.
Bias: AI systems are trained on large datasets, which are created by humans.
Consequently, these systems can inadvertently learn and perpetuate the biases present in the training data.
Common biases include: Cognitive biases: Unconscious errors in human judgment that can inuence AI training and outcomes.
Implicit bias: Unconscious stereotypes that can be reected in AI if present in training data.
Gender, racial, and age bias: AI decisions can unfairly disadvantage people based on these factors.
Sampling bias: Occurs when training data isnt representative of the population.
Temporal bias: Occurs when training data is time-specic and may not reect current or future states.
Over-tting to training data: Occurs when AI performs well on training data but poorly on new data.
is happens when a model captures external factors (noise) that alter the data points but are not part of the system that we want to model along with the underlying pattern in the training data, resulting in poor performance on new data.
Algorithmic bias: Prejudiced assumptions made during algorithm development can lead to biased outcomes.
" Explainability: AI models, including LLMs, are oen referred to as black # boxes because its challenging to understand how they arrive at a particular $ output.
is lack of transparency applies in particular to neural networks % and deep learning systems whereas simple ML algorithms like decision trees & allow an external user to understand how the AI came to a specic result.
' ( ) Scalability: AI systems that perform at scale in production settings can become 0 very expensive and very hard to maintain for a number of related reasons: 1 2 3 Data sourcing and ingesting: In general, large AI models perform better 4 when more good quality data is used to train them.
Large, good quality 5 datasets are dicult and expensive to handle and store.
6 7 8 Compute cost to train: Most deep learning systems, and LLMs in particular, 9 require vast amounts of compute power to train, with periodic retraining @ to maintain acceptable performances or to add more recent data.
A B C Compute cost to D operate: Running a model that serves many concurrent E users can become complex and expensive in exponential ways.
F Copyright and AI A complex, critical issue linked to current limitations of AI is that of copyright infringement.
As I write this book, in 2024, it is still unclear how the issues of consent, credit, and compensation can be addressed and resolved.
Most authors argue that before an LLM is trained using their original work, they should be consulted to give permission.
en, when their work contributes to an LLM output, they should be credited and compensated.
Most AI companies argue that its impossible to correctly attribute credit since LLMs are not explainable and its impossible to determine what data source is used to generate arbitrary output.
ey also argue that LLMs and generative AI in general are not dissimilar to artists who draw inspiration and build on the work of those before them.
Its dicult to predict where the copyright law is going to settle, but recently large publishing companies and AI organizations have signed contracts to regulate the usage of copyright-protected material.
It 15 seems likely that more of those deals will be struck to try and create a viable business model.
e issue of copyright is gaining momentum.
However, there are conicting and contradictory regulations across dierent regions.
As an area of ongoing change and development, business leaders are advised to refer to the latest regulations in the regions where their organizations operate.
h Trustworthy AI Trustworthy AI is also an emerging eld originating from collaborative international eorts, privacy-enhancing technologies, and ethical principles, aimed at reliable, transparent, and privacy-respecting AI systems.
It has become an integral part of the AI system lifecycle to ensure benecial and reliable outcomes.
It requires governance and regulatory compliance, from ideation to design, development, deployment, and machine learning operations.
v Generative AI and LLMs in more detail LLMs, a subset of generative AI, are good at summarizing context and language, which is particularly eective in text-based writing assistance, content creation, and reasoning over structured and unstructured data.
Companies are applying LLMs across dierent sectors to enhance their operations and we have in-depth case studies where we discuss these applications.
e common emerging applications for generative AI include in operations, enabling a new type of customer engagement and care, empowering advisors and market researchers, building co-pilots to help customers nd knowledge faster, improving marketing eectiveness, and accelerating product development.
Foundational components of LLMs Tokens and vectors, embeddings, and transformers are all interconnected concepts and play crucial roles in processing and understanding natural language data.
ey help LLMs to grasp the intricacies of human language 16 and perform a wide range of Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks.
Heres a brief explanation of each.
Tokens and Vectors In the rst stage of the process of understanding human language, the text data is broken down into smaller units called tokens, which can be words, parts of words, or punctuation marks.
ese tokens are then transformed into vectors, which are numerical representations of these words.
is transformation process allows the model to understand the meaning of words and how they relate to each other.
Embeddings e vectors created from tokens are also known as embeddings, which make text, images, and audio digestible for AI transformers.
Embeddings are numerical vectors that represent, in a machine-readable format, the semantic context of a text (for chatbots) in a format that is language-independent and designed to retain as much knowledge as possible.
For example, the machine will be able to parse the token Florence as a city (the Italian city in Tuscany) or a rst name (as in Florence Nightingale).
is allows the machine to retain the implied knowledge that in a specic text might be describing the architecture of a city as opposed to the founder of modern nursing.
A key feature of embeddings is that similar concepts have similar values.
is feature enables search, comparison, and manipulation beyond simple full-text queries, thereby enhancing the capabilities of AI systems.
Transformers e transformer is the core engine of LLMs.
Its primary function is to convert an input embedding into an output embedding, which is then translated into human language.
e transformer leverages all its training to determine the most probable output given the users input.
It generates its output by selecting the most likely token at each step.
is process allows the transformer to produce 17 coherent and contextually appropriate responses.
Its a key component in the eectiveness of LLMs in understanding and generating human-like text.
The evolution and impact of transformers in AI In 2017, a paper titled Attention Is All You Need was published by Google and introduced the concept of transformers.
is kick-started an entirely new era of AI.
Transformers now form the backbone of many cutting-edge AI applications enabling the scaling of LLMs like those that power ChatGPT and Gemini.
While LLMs focus primarily on language, generative AI models can serve other purposes, transforming data between multiple modalities, including text, images, video, and audio.
Regardless of the purpose, each model interprets the underlying meaning of its input and generates the most likely output according to the training data.
e real power of LLMs comes from the fact that they work in areas far beyond language.
e latest release of AI solutions is multimodal, meaning that they can use a text prompt to generate an image or video.
Examples of multimodal systems include: H I P DALL-E, an OpenAI Q system that can create images from text descriptions, R using a large neural network trained on a dataset of text-image pairs.
S T U Midjourney, a generative articial intelligence program and service that can V create images from text descriptions (prompts).
W X Y Stable Diusion AI, an open-source AI model that can generate realistic ` images from text descriptions or other inputs.
It uses a technique called latent a diusion, which involves the iterative addition of noise random variations b or distortions to an image until it matches the desired output.
c d e SORA OpenAI system generates videos from text prompts, due for launch f in 2024.
18 e current LLMs available on the market, like GPT, are pre-trained on a diverse range of internet text and can be used by many users at the same time.
However, they are not designed to learn or adapt to new information aer training.
is means that while they can generate creative text based on the input provided, they cant be ne-tuned on a specic task without going through a re-training process.
For instance, if you have a specic set of documents on a specialized topic and want to teach an LLM to understand and generate text about this topic, you cannot do this with models like ChatGPT.
Instead, you would need to take a general-purpose LLM and train it on your specic dataset.
is process, known as ne-tuning, adapts the model to your specic needs.
However, ne-tuning a model is technologically complex and expensive.
e investment is typically justied by the potential return or by addressing concerns such as security and privacy that require the model to have a deep understanding of a particular domain.
A practical example of a ne-tuned LLM is a chatbot trained on the FAQ of a large enterprise.
is chatbot can answer specic questions about the companys products or processes, providing valuable assistance to both customers and employees.
Grounded Generation/Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) Grounded Generation, or Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG), is a secure, cost-eective alternative to ne-tuning in LLMs.
It reduces hallucination levels and uses semantic retrieval techniques for context provision.
It can provide citations, enhancing the credibility of its outputs.
RAG is adept at extracting, encoding, and indexing intelligence from various sources, which is then retrieved and ranked per user queries.
It generates relevant content by considering unique contexts and historical materials.
e choice between Grounded Generation and ne-tuning depends on the task requirements.
Some projects may benet from a combination of RAG and ne-tuning, enhancing model performance and reliability.
is trade-o is explained in depth in the Vectara case study ( Chapter 26 ).

is open-source nature is leading to diverse and innovative applications of these models, as individuals and organizations take them in unique and interesting directions, as we will see in the following interviews and case studies.
e pace of development in this area is likely to be rapid, with companies striving to create use cases that oer some form of dierentiation.
is competitive landscape is expected to drive investment, innovation, and progress in the eld.
How controlled your industry domain is will heavily inuence how applicable the models will be.
In areas of very high regulation, such as in nance and !
healthcare, where being correct " versus close enough is most important, these # models are less applicable without considerable investment in customization.
b AI Technology Stack % An AI technology & structure, oen referred to as the stack, is a set of tools and ' technologies that are used to develop, deploy, and manage AI applications.
It ( consists of dierent layers that perform various functions such as data collection, ) data processing, machine learning, model training, model deployment, and 0 user interface.
An AI tech stack can vary depending on the type and complexity 1 of the AI project.
20 2 To understand the components of the tech stack, lets use an example of an 3 infrastructure investment rm wanting to forecast the value of their assets at 4 exit.
To accomplish this, the rm will need the following: Data sources: Both internal (for example, asset depreciation history) and external (for example, macroeconomic factors, demographic data) sources to provide the necessary data.
Data pipeline layer: Automated processes to ingest data from various sources and store it consistently in a central data store.
Data storage: A system to eciently store and potentially transform the data to meet enterprise needs.
Data quality: Ensure datasets are of good enough quality to properly train the AI model.
AI algorithm: Use the data from the storage to train a forecasting model to predict asset value at exit.
Past asset valuations and market conditions contribute to the training of the algorithm.
AI platform: Provide a toolset to eciently build, test, and deploy AI algorithms in an enterprise, shared environment.
e toolset includes Integrated Development Environments (IDEs), code editors, debuggers, testing tools, version control systems, and documentation tools.
APIs (application programming interface): Forecast data is accessible programmatically through an internal system.
APIs allow authorized third- party systems to access the data in a controlled and monitored way.
AI solution: A fully integrated solution that allows business users to explore and analyze forecasted asset valuations as well as what if scenarios to anticipate various outcomes.
It oen includes a web-based interface for interaction and a dashboard for standardized reporting.

e rst was pattern recognition AI R applications, which have been pivotal in detecting fraud, identifying quality S issues in products or pipelines, and diagnosing diseases.

h LISA i : How is generative AI dierent from the previous AI wave, and what p benets do you see it bringing?
q AMR r : Generative AI s is transformative t because, for the rst time in history, u we have AI systems that can interpret human language as we do, which is v unprecedented.
It represents a milestone in the user interface between humans w and soware.
x e analogy I share about this moment is the leap from command-line interfaces y to Windows 95 and then the iPhone.
Originally, the only way we could interact with soware was through something called the command line.
If you are old enough to remember DOS, then you remember all the commands and options you had to put aer the commands.
Its a xed set of words and you A A MR A A WADA ll LL AH 38 have to use them in that way.

e forms were a lot more intuitive with nice user interface modalities, which open up the consumption of soware for consumers or inside of businesses, or to a bigger class of people.
However, we literally had to rewrite all of our soware to adapt every single application that worked in the command line to work in this new way.
at was the rst phase of how we evolved our interaction with soware.
e following phase was the iPhone and touch screens, which opened up the consumption of technology to many more people because everybody knows how to use their ngers.
However, it also meant, again, that we had to rewrite every single application that we used both in and outside of the enterprise.

Today, generative AI technologies allow us to interact with soware in the same way we interact with each other.

e consumption will become ubiquitous; everybody will be able to use soware to do their tasks.

ats what we are about enabling and asking, What are the benets of this wave?

Whatever used to take us hours or days to do, well now be able to do in minutes.

JO s S HUA R R UBIN 98 G G H Pre-empting model drift H : When algorithms underperform or the world H changes in a H way the model wasnt prepared for, this model dri can lead H to lost revenue and pose risks.
ese can be caused by a number of factors, H including economic shis, new customer behaviors, and seasonal variations.
H is can lead to less accurate predictions because the model is trying to apply H outdated rules to a new situation.

SARVARTH M M I s S RA 122 Embedded AI for secure-centric models : Embedded AI uses techniques that keep customer data secure while leveraging it to address specic customer needs.
Engineering methods like RAG, RL, and prompt engineering personalize the experience for each user, and dedicated computing resources ensure this data remains private and isnt used to train external models.
Over time, general models can be improved by incorporating trends identied in customer- specic models within a structured system that maintains data separation.
is approach allows for continuous improvement based on customer feedback and trends while safeguarding the unique nature of customer-centric models.

underpinning operations by identifying specic, routine tasks within your !

N N ATHA l L IE G G AVEAU 153 Key Takeaways Extracting value from data : Deriving value from data requires a strategic and organized approach, managed by a team who understands its value and can ensure its proper use.
Merely having vast amounts of data is insucient; leveraging it for actionable insights is crucial.
Early diagnostic for AI opportunities : To assess how well data informs current decision-making across the organization, start by asking questions about key areas like customer segmentation, promotions, and supply chain.
is initial exploration will quickly uncover high value opportunities for further investigation.
Phased approach : Designing pilots and achieving small wins can help get buy- in from the team.
Real-world success stories can help convince stakeholders and accelerate the adoption process.
Learning from failure : Failures can provide valuable lessons.
For instance, a failed pilot program to automate product pricing can highlight the need to review the whole go-to-market strategy and start again.
Dual approach to empowering the whole organization : e successful application of AI requires clear executive directives in a top-down approach and a comprehensive change management program.
Active participation from every level of the organization in a bottom-up approach is also essential.
Its crucial to ensure teams understand the benets of new tools or solutions, how to use them, and the improvements they can bring to their work.

LOUI s S D D ICE s S ARI 179 Key Takeaways Leading AI transformation : Empathize with stakeholders and end users, showing how data analytics and AI can benet them in tangible ways, by solving problems or enhancing outcomes.
Prioritize the value and speed to value of data projects, balancing short-term wins with long-term bets.
Assess the impact and feasibility of each project, allocating resources and time accordingly.
Work with physical products and supply chains where AI can accelerate and streamline pricing, promotion, forecasting, and inventory management.
Be innovative, using AI to create new products and services that meet customer needs.
Data and AI best practices : Data quality is not a prerequisite for initiating data projects, but a continuous process that evolves over time.
Focus on using the minimum amount of quality data needed to start impacting a business problem and build from there, rather than spending too much time on cleaning and validating data.
Collaborate with dierent divisions and ensure they have access to the data they require for their projects, and sometimes allocate funds within the company to facilitate this.
Structure a cross-functional squad that focuses on specic domains, such as pricing, demand planning, and e-commerce.
Working in two-week sprints, follow an agile methodology to ensure ecient incremental delivery.
Data products : Create data products that can be used by other parts of the organization, such as price elasticity models, demand forecasting tools, and personalized customer experiences.
Make them user-friendly and accessible, and integrate them into the existing systems and processes.
Measure the results and impact of work, using independent neutral groups such as nance for validation.
An agile approach : Adapt products and recommendations to suit the needs and preferences of sellers and customers.
Focus on business results, rather than complex innovations.
However, be aware of advancements, such as generative AI, computer vision, and 3D rendering, that can oer new possibilities and solutions for your industry or sector.
Accept the fact that nobody has any patience, so demonstrate incremental progress to secure investments.

Key Takeaways Value over hype : Technology has evolved into a key driver and contributor for business value.
Understanding both the hype cycle and value cycle for technology and data analytics is crucial for creating new products and insights.
Its important to take a capability approach, executing smaller projects before taking on bigger ones, evidencing value impact.
Data-driven decision-making : Using data analytics to provide actionable intelligence is crucial to creating targeted management programs.
is approach enhances existing processes and informs the development of further improvement programs.
From raw data to targeted solutions : Data availability opens up opportunities to develop targeted products or services tailored to a particular user base.
e initial challenge, however, lies in addressing data quality issues.
Scattered data in various formats needs to be normalized and cleaned up to ensure its usability for subsequent analysis and operations.
V V I c C KEY R R ODRIGUE s S 187 Scaling up with speed : e increase in technology and computer capacity has made it easier and faster to roll out projects.
e availability of algorithms with cloud service providers makes it quick and easy to scale projects.
Running a mini hackathon can help identify partners with the relevant technical resources and skills to tackle the problems.
Be ready to handle failures and dont rely on only one vendor or partner.
Leverage the cloud for faster solutions : Cloud-based cognitive services can signicantly accelerate your time to market for AI solutions.

@ @ @ Harness resistance and creativity @ : To overcome challenges associated with A individuals time, attention, and willingness to engage, it is crucial to cultivate A an environment that encourages open dialogue.

Key Takeaways : Consider the Chief Data Ocer as a signicant role in the organization, beyond just managing data.
is role can be a strategic accelerator, leading the transformation of data into value to drive the organizations growth and innovation, managing risks, and enhancing agility and competitive advantage.
Building a data-driven culture : Emphasize data literacy, foster a culture of data-driven decision-making, have a clear vision and commitment.
ese considerations are more important than just focusing on technology and need to be championed by the executive team, especially when budget allocations are being discussed.
An eective operating model involves having the right people interacting in the right way.
is includes having data scientists, IT managers, and directors of engineering working together as part of the same team.
Prioritize data governance : Address poor data governance urgently because a lack of trust in the data can lead to internal friction.
Demonstrate to the Chief Risk Ocer and the CEO how data governance can be a solution to data risk.
Prioritize data governance alongside addressing urgent commercial matters.
Internal training : Empower employees and enhance change management with a program of training.
Start with a basic introduction to data and lead to AI projects.
Train employees on how to identify a use case, create a problem statement, and start collecting data.
PETER JA c C K s S ON 219 : Collaborate closely with the CEO, the COO, CPO, and CCO to set priorities, which are translated directly into the team.
Be exible, continuously review business process ows, and redesign if necessary.

Power of storytelling : Tell a compelling story about what is possible to inspire colleagues and prioritize issues.

Run scenarios of potential outcomes to demonstrate how a model might impact decision-making.
Anticipate potential legal and operational challenges that probabilistic models can introduce, and plan mitigation strategies beforehand.
Future-proof executive support : Build a broad executive understanding of the nancial impact and proposed AI solutions to gain buy-in.
Recognize that the development and implementation of AI solutions oen require time and investment.
Secure this wide support to mitigate the disruption risk of key stakeholders moving to dierent roles.
Data consent and legal compliance : Understand the industrys specic regulatory and enforcement challenges.
Secure explicit consent from customers to use their data for a specic initiative.
Ensure strict segregation from other datasets and that it is only used for the specied initiative.
: Consider the trade-o between model accuracy and applicability.
While a model might be highly accurate for a specic set of data, it may not have a broader application.

269 G The Response s Amr and his team at Vectara have developed an end-to-end approach to help t companies u like SonoSim leverage powerful generative AI technologies using their v own data.

Phase 2 : Implementing Vectaras platform and calibrating it to SonoSims environment .
Vectaras Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)-based generative AI cloud-based platform delivered a natural language search capability.
RAG improves the accuracy of generative AI models by fetching facts from, and grounding the model on external knowledge sources.
e conversational interface enabled interaction with real patient cases, delivering a hyper-personalized learning experience, custom course creation with continuous assessments, skill development, and mastery for medical professionals that overcame the previous nancial, linguistic, and logistical barriers.

b Phase 2: Implementing Vectaras Platform and Calibrating to b SonoSims Environment ' The Vectara platform and process ( AMR ) : Vectaras platform 0 is at the forefront 1 of generative AI and neural 2 network 3 4 advancements, providing exceptional search accuracy through semantic 5 understanding.
Our system excels in deciphering user queries, regardless of 6 phrasing, ensuring the delivery of highly pertinent results.
7 For comparison, OpenAI and other models facilitate the ne-tuning of large 8 language models.
is process involves uploading proprietary data to iteratively 274 9 train a model, thus customizing its responses.
Although user-friendly and @ ecacious, this approach is not without its challenges.
A Fine-tuning begins with a base model, such as GPT-3 or GPT-4, already trained B on publicly available datasets and various corporate contributions.
e objective C for businesses is to embed their unique dataset into the model.
By doing so, the D model evolves, integrating the new data to form a derivative, ne-tuned version, E now enriched with both public and proprietary knowledge.
F In this process, companies strive to exclude unwanted data through a technique G called negative training.
is involves instructing the LLM to disregard certain H information.
However, completely eliminating unwanted data is dicult.
I ere might always be some residual data le behind, especially if there isnt a P perfect understanding of what to exclude.
To address this challenge, the new, Q customer-specic data is incorporated, not in its original form but as condensed, R meaningful interpretations learned by the model.
is way, the LLM leverages S valuable insights while minimizing the inuence of unwanted elements.
T Fine-tunings main drawbacks are its cost and computational demands.
Its an U intensive process that requires powerful hardware, like the NVIDIA A100 Tensor V Core GPU, designed to provide acceleration for AI and analytics.
It can take W extensive time for the data to be assimilated, making it impractical for rapidly X changing datasets.
Y Another concern is the potential for hallucinations.
Even when ne-tuned, ` these language models lack strict mechanisms to prevent such errors, leading a to occasional unreliable responses.
In sectors where accuracy is critical, such as b healthcare or nance, the inability to trace the models reasoning process or cite c data sources is a signicant drawback.
d Furthermore, even with careful training, bias and the inadvertent inclusion of e copyrighted material remain risks, opening the door to, for example, a copyright f owner coming aer me instead of going aer OpenAI, because Im in a well-known g brand using it.
Intellectual property violations are a big concern for companies h leveraging these models.
275 i In contrast, Vectara uses Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) to mitigate p these issues.
is method reduces costs, enhances security, lowers the risk of q hallucinations, and allows for the provision of citations.
It eciently extracts, r encodes, and s indexes content t from diverse formats (including PDFs, PowerPoint, u images, video, and audio sources), aligning search results closely with user inquiries.
v It also enables the generation of contextually relevant and precise content.
w Figure 26.
1 Flow diagram demonstrating how grounded generation works x Our neural search technology, integrating hybrid search (keyword and LLM), y signicantly outperforms traditional keyword-based searches in delivering relevant results.
is is because the engine operates not just on the recognition of keywords, but also on the comprehension of the meaning behind the words.
e integration of hybrid search enhances the precision and context-awareness of the results.
is multilingual capability also means it has a broad geographical reach.
RAG is an approach that leverages the power of LLMs to generate responses.
e key aspect of this approach is that it retrieves relevant information from external data sources in addition to the users data.
is combined knowledge base allows RAG to tailor responses that are essentially a reection of the users 276 unique knowledge base, making the interaction more relevant.
ats really the secret sauce here.
is is all made possible by advanced information retrieval systems and machine learning models that prioritize semantic correlation over keyword matching.
is semantic approach mirrors the cognitive processing of LLMs, utilizing vector embeddings to represent meaning.

2 Vectaras end-to-end platform for retrieval, reranking, encoding, and generation is novel technique is grounded in research, including papers authored by my co-founder, Amin Ahmad, which describe the development of neural networks that transition from linguistic to semantic comprehension.
Despite currently utilizing GPT models as a foundation, we anticipate transitioning to Meta AIs LLaMA, a new suite of LLMs unveiled in 2023.
277 Translating language into meaning Vectaras platform revolutionizes data processing by translating it into a vector database, essentially a meaning database.
is preserves the high-delity content, converting it into vectors that encapsulate the essence behind words, in whatever language you use.
ey are stored in their original content not compressed or ne-tuned.
So, when a query comes in, the engine matches the querys semantic intent with its knowledge base, ensuring only relevant, high- quality facts are used to construct responses.
Eliminating bias and enhancing accuracy Our RAG approach prevents biases and copyright infringements by strictly using veried facts.
It eliminates hallucinations, as responses are based on concrete !
data, not conjecture.
is method is supported by advanced meaning engines, " crucial for accurate information retrieval and ranking, thus empowering the # LLM to generate precise, accurate, and $ reliable % answers.
& Responsiveness, balance, and copyright adherence ' e process starts ( when a ) query is input into our 0 search box.
e system then 1 collates results and synthesizes them into a coherent response.
Crucially, the LLM 2 is restricted to using only the provided facts, which are pre-validated and scored, 3 based on whether the meaning matches that of the prompts, with 100% indicating 4 a perfect match and 0% indicating no relevance.
e model is instructed to cite 5 these facts, employing a citation notation that reects their relevance score.
If none 6 of the facts are pertinent, the model is programmed to refrain from fabricating 7 information and instead admit to the lack of relevant data.
8 is method ensures a multi-perspective approach to information processing.
9 When dealing with queries that inherently contain multiple viewpoints or possible @ answers, the system is designed to present a balanced view, incorporating all A relevant perspectives rather than favoring any single one.
278 B In addition, the model is constrained to only reproduce facts, which means it C will not inadvertently disclose any copyrighted material unless it forms part of D the facts it has been supplied.
While the model may learn and replicate certain E writing styles, it is our responsibility to ensure that these do not infringe on F copyright laws.
G Citations for transparency H Last but not least, citations I play a critical role in the transparency of our process.
P Citations linked to the facts ensure explainability, allowing users to trace the Q origin of the information provided by the LLM.
is signicantly reduces R hallucinations as the model consistently grounds its responses in factual data.
S Google and Microso also have ground generation.
T The meaning engine U e key component V to RAGs eectiveness is the capability of the meaning engine.
W If you dont nd the right facts, the model collapses.
Its wholly dependent on the X facts being correctly ordered, based on their meaning and being scored correctly.
Y is precision enables the LLM to perform adeptly even when presented with ` data it has not been previously trained on because of the strength of our zero-shot a model learning capacity.
Because it indexes new information, discerning the best b meanings and making informed associations, it can deal with data categories or c classes its never encountered d before.
is advanced capability is foundational e to our LLMs ability.
f Specialization through verticalization g As we broaden h our LLMs verticalization, we tailor them to sectors like legal, i medical, and accounting, while maintaining a general model for wider topics.
p Real-time prompt analysis helps us understand the customers intent and guide q queries to the most suitable specialized model.
is focused strategy guarantees r not only accurate but also contextually relevant responses, leveraging the specic s knowledge of the selected vertical.
279 t Simplifying integration u If you were building v this yourself, you would have to worry about all of these steps.

Key Learnings Evolution of interfaces : e evolution of soware has gone through several phases, transitioning from command-line interfaces to graphical user interfaces, such as Windows 95, and subsequently to touch interfaces like those on the iPhone.
Each phase brought soware to a broader audience but necessitated the rewriting of applications to suit new modalities.
Generative AI has now enabled AI systems to comprehend human language, marking a pivotal advancement in the interface between humans and soware.
282 Data compression : Similar to how the human brain compresses information, LLMs are trained on vast datasets while requiring only a fraction of the original data for storage.
is data compression is valuable, but for company-specic data, referencing the original source is crucial.
e model (X) processes the information, while another entity (Y) holds the actual facts.
Hallucinations : AI hallucinations refer to inaccurate or misleading results generated by AI models.
Fine-tuning : e current LLMs available on the market, like GPT, are pre- trained on a diverse range of internet text and can be used by many users at the same time.
is means that, while they can generate creative text based on the input provided, they cant be ne-tuned on a specic task without going through a re-training process.
Fine-tuning builds on pre-trained LLMs by incorporating a companys unique data.
is creates a customized model enriched with both public and proprietary knowledge.
Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) : RAG is a secure, cost-eective alternative to ne-tuning in LLMs.
Its adept at extracting, encoding, and indexing intelligence from various sources, which is then retrieved and ranked per user queries.
e choice between RAG and ne-tuning depends on the task requirements.
Knowledge retrieval : LLMs signicantly improve the eciency and accuracy of knowledge retrieval within organizations, enabling swi and precise querying of proprietary information.
LLMs can help bridge gaps in specialist education, especially where there are various learning modalities, high competency thresholds, limited time and resources, as well as teaching opportunities.
283 Trustworthy AI : Trustworthy AI is a rapidly evolving eld focused on developing reliable, transparent, and privacy-respecting AI systems.
In healthcare specically, trustworthy AI is crucial for maintaining the integrity of medical education and practice.
It addresses ethical concerns like patient privacy, the explainability of AI decisions, and minimizing bias.
Future-proof education : As medical knowledge continues to rapidly expand, integrating AI ensures that educational content remains current and relevant.
e ability to update and disseminate new ndings quickly is key to future- proong the education of medical professionals.

Context-dependent issues x Addressing existing global platforms bias toward English-speaking digital natives y HARSH : We can oen take a digitized life for granted, but there are millions of people around the world who are just beginning their online lives.
For instance, hundreds of millions of people who speak only Indian languages like Hindi, Tamil, Telugu, Kannada may have access to WhatsApp and Facebook, but are not widely exposed to micro-blogging or social media where they can express themselves in their own language.
e handful of global players that dominate the social media landscape leave non-English speaking communities underserved.

HARSH : In the early stages of launching a social media platform, if there arent enough users interested in a particular topic, such as sports, then even if a sports celebrity joins, they might not nd an audience.
is lack of engagement could discourage them from creating more content.
erefore, its crucial to have a strategy to identify and onboard content creators who are relevant to the users interests.

290 Approach A three-phase approach was taken.
Phase 1: Determining prioritization and design Feature development and language prioritization HARSH : My team set out to build a new feature, which we named Topics, that would allow users to explicitly state their interests, for example, entertainment, sports, or politics, when onboarding.
We needed a way to map users to interesting proles and determine what types of celebrities, inuencers, and thought leaders to onboard, which in turn would generate and present relevant content to our users.
When faced with a project like this, the key rst step when focusing on speed and eciency is to reduce the complexity.
So, one of the dicult decisions I had to make at the start was to choose between taking the feature (Topics) live in all languages versus the top one or two by volume of users, which were Hindi and English.
While we may have lost momentum on other languages, the decision to prioritize these two was the right one because if wed done it all together, we !
would have delayed the entire launch.
Building it in one or two priority languages " enabled us to scale it out aerward.
# Data labeling and quality assurance $ HARSH % : While English and Hindi were prioritized, the team trained LLMs & on human-labeled data to categorize Koo content into topics across 10 Indian ' languages and 10 international languages.
( To get the data to the right level of readiness, we deployed an in-house team to ) review and manually label content in a process called human-in-the-loop (HITL).
0 Our in-house team collaborated with our community leaders (who build and 1 manage our language communities).
Over several weeks, one leader from each 2 language category reviewed and manually labeled the content.
291 3 We developed Topic classication using Natural Language Understanding (NLU) 4 technologies.
ese technologies include LLMS, for example, BERT, and more 5 recently ne-tuned open source LLMs like Metas Liama2.
6 is process produced a few thousand pieces of labeled content per category, 7 which was our gold standard dataset.
is was used to train the model, which 8 was then used to classify the content on the platform.
Oen, in this process, the 9 models cant classify some content with certainty, so its then taken back to the @ original auditor to provide a clear label.
is quality assurance helps the model A improve over time.
B Open source collaboration for model development with NLP C HARSH D : To enable Topics for Indian languages where Natural Language Processing E (NLP) research was F nascent, we collaborated with top Indian research groups G like the Indian Institute of Technology (IIT), Chennai, and AI4Bharat to come H up with a solution.
I I am very proud to say that our language model (nicknamed KooBERT) was P open sourced on Hugging Face to spur further research.

is means giving them the output that they would see b on the platform if they were users.
en, get their feedback.

Some things we learned included: Role of human observation : Unless we looked at the output of any model or algorithm, we didnt ship it; we didnt take it to production.
Of course, standard model evaluation metrics, (accuracy, false positives, ROC curves, and so on) are fundamental.

Creating a set of internal power users : Every time we had a new algorithm or feature release, we checked the feature among employees, across our teams (Data Ops, QA, and ML) who were all engaged users of the platform.
ey test a feature pre-launch as a user would experience it.
is gave us a powerful pre-launch feedback loop.

While we are always very metrics-driven (with the model, engagement, KPIs, product performance, and so on), this human element of quality assurance tells us stories; for example, we learned that there was a strong sub-group that was pleased to nd poets on the platform, so this became an interest area we introduced on Koo.

uYsHia&5X,zW8eBaE'c+79Rz:)s:+92sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)s:+92s:+92sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)s:+92sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)sI:)s:+92sI:)sI:)sI: 294 Phase 3: Deploying models and safeguards Using open source to help launch multiple models at scale HARSH : One of the key considerations with this business challenge was one of scale.
In the beginning, it wasnt clear how we could deploy LLMs in production so we could serve millions of users at low latency levels.
I think the science was there, we knew how to do it, but we wanted to do it rapidly and push six to ten models into production for the launch of a single language.
And, as is oen the case with high-growth companies, we didnt have a very large team (at that point, there were three or four people on the machine learning team, albeit with a mandate to grow).
To solve this problem, we used open source soware from Uber called Ludwig for rapid model training.
Additionally, BentoML was used for MLOps and model deployments, with the teams working closely to integrate contributions and innovations for rapid deployment.
Im very proud that a lot of the work we did with the open source community made " great leaps with the Indian language NLP.
Working with BERT, a foundational # technology used in building LLMs that Google came out with in 2018 before $ GPT improved search performance enormously.
Using BERT-based language % models, we open sourced our BERT language model that was trained on Indian & language and its now available for anyone to go and use.
' We also continue to have regular brainstorms and workshops with the teams ( so they can share their ideas and insights on how to continue to leverage open ) source technology eectively.
0 Continuing to contribute to the open source community not only enhances the 1 companys reputation at the forefront of technological advancements but also 2 helps foster innovation.
3 Coordinating with DevOps and back-end teams 4 HARSH 5 : Tight collaboration between DevOps and back-end teams was also 6 crucial to get our resources in order and ensure we were being frugal in terms 7 of our cloud costs.
We had to be mindful about how we used GPUs and CPUs, 295 8 when to run in real time versus batch processing (the latter is more economical 9 in terms of costs), how do we use CPUs?
When do we actually run it in real time?
@ When can we do batch processing and save on costs?
A Launch and initial scale B HARSH C : So, we initially took Topics live in English, because a lot of the machine D learning, NLP technology for extracting keywords for classifying content in E various categories for training models is very mature for English.
In Hindi, we F had to struggle a little bit and thats where we innovated.
G We worked closely with the team at AI4Baharat, Indias most sophisticated NLP H research group, supported by Nandan Nilekani, who is almost like Indias CTO.
I We leveraged a lot of the work the team had published in research journals to P develop our Topics in not just Hindi but eight more Indian languages, including Q Tamil, Telugu, Kannada, Marathi, Assamese.
We took it live in our product, R which gave us a lot of great engagement S with time spent on the app increasing T as a metric that we tracked.
U Safeguards V HARSH W : Ensuring content security and moderation is something were passionate X about at Koo.
Everyone involved in the business wants to build Koo as a safe Y haven for our family members to use, so weve been very thorough with our ` safeguards creating the following models: & & & Legal compliance & : We are fully compliant with the laws of every country we & operate in, such as the IT Act in India, which is akin to abiding by banking laws.
& & & Protection of personal data & : Our Personally Identiable Information (PII) is ' encrypted and accessed strictly on a need-to-know basis, subject to rigorous ' checks and balances, and usually involves our founders for particularly ' sensitive cases.
296 ' ' ' Content moderation ' : Weve implemented robust measures to instantly ' remove NSFW content.
Nudity and violence are not permitted in videos, ' prole pictures, or any uploads.
Our system is designed to detect and delete ' such content within milliseconds.
( ( ( Toxicity and hate speech regulation ( : Weve developed anti-toxicity models ( to identify and remove hate speech, based on legal denitions rather than our ( discretion.
We use both automated systems for clear-cut cases and human ( oversight for more nuanced situations.
( ( ( Transparency ) : We maintain a transparency center to report the number of ) takedowns, whether due to government orders or violations of our platform rules.
e safety of Koo is a mission deeply personal to me, especially as Im heavily involved in developing the machine learning that powers these systems.
) Thoughts on hiring and developing talent HARSH : In addition to all the product-related learnings that came out of the development, build, and launch of Topics for Koo, I would like to share some specic insights on hiring and developing talent for startups.
Who to target Given the high demand for the data science skillset, like many leaders, I have faced challenges in hiring suitably experienced candidates because its not possible to compete with much larger, well-funded companies when it comes to compensation.
With this in mind, we oen look for relatively junior but motivated technical developers to join the team, focusing on nding passionate self-starters who are aligned with Koos mission.
I developed an interview process focused on practical assessments like reviewing/ building models hands-on, to gauge immediate technical skills.
I frequently ask candidates to submit ideas for features they would build if hired to lter for those invested in Koo.
In my view, this is a particularly strong indicator because 297 someone who is motivated and has spent time deeply exploring the platform and coming up with ideas for high-impact features is highly likely to emerge as a strong future contributor to the team.
Developing and retaining talent Hiring is just the rst step.
To develop and retain talent, I pair junior hires with senior team members as mentors to help them upskill and transition from amateur coders to professional developers.
I have also instituted structured and deliberate processes like writing !
design docs and retrospectives to promote " continuous learning.
2 Results and Impact $ HARSH % : Aer rigorous & assessment and QA, the Topics feature was launched in ' Hindi and English rst, delivering a minimum viable product to gather initial ( feedback.
e accelerated launch increased time spent on Koo by 100% for users ) that adopted it.
With Topics established, the team could then scale the feature 0 across all 20 languages.
1 Topics was key for Koo to build thriving, localized communities and personalize 2 the experience.
Understanding the popularity of Topics enabled Koo to onboard 3 relevant celebrities, thought leaders, and creators that users wanted to follow.
4 Advanced features like personalized recommendations based on user anities 5 further connected users with relevant voices to follow.
6 e visibility Topics provided into audience interests also helped creators better 7 reach a more targeted audience and encouraged them to create more engaging 8 content.

W Phase 1: Scoping the requirements y PAT : Lots of businesses recognize the need for automated systems (chatbot or virtual agents) to reduce costs.
Many have deployed self-help platforms, which are essentially knowledge search bars, but, as weve found, these oen fail to provide valuable help as people dont like to read instructions (they prefer things to be fully automated), so employees will just pick up the phone and call the service desk.
ChatGPT has changed employee expectations around how virtual agents are supposed to work, leading to more complex and varied queries.
However, what I repeatedly see is a lack of skills to deliver them because of a complete misunderstanding of the eort level that it takes to build a language model that really understands human language.
A recent study by an analyst rm found 25% of the cost is the build and 75% is the maintenance.
And I can vouch that this is true.
Its not just building the bot; its also the continuous improvement needed on it.
So, our philosophy out of the gate was how can we build a virtual agent on a conversational platform that really understands the language of employees?
We built the language model and then continued to improve it by focusing on that use case.
307 ChatGPT has also led to a desire for companies to expand to enterprise search; however, these technologies oen lack the context of the enterprise and its tools, leading to incorrect answers.
So, Espressive focuses on higher volume, low- hanging fruit when we come into an organization, doing an analysis of their top call drivers.
Focus on commonality PAT : e virtual agent is agnostic because what we ended up discovering was that it doesnt matter who you work for; 85% of what employees ask for when calling the service desk is the same.
Each business will have its unique lingo, acronyms, or buzzwords, but the things that theyre looking for, the problems that theyre experiencing, are universally the same.
For example, I need a new laptop, I need access to a piece of soware, What are the company vacations?
, I have a question about my retirement plan.
Its all the same.
So, focusing on a domain that was universally the same across all of these dierent companies provided us with an opportunity to really focus on improving our language model so that when we get deployed, we have a high degree of understanding.
And then, of course, with additional oversight on supervised capabilities, we continue to improve that over time.

s For example, we recently ran a training session for a pharmaceutical company.

318 The Business Challenge DAVID : A leading consumer goods company came to us to help them leverage AI around research.
e request was framed pretty much around, Can you write some prompts for us that will enable us to do product innovation eectively in an automated way?
but they didnt say the word automation.
I wasnt aligned to full automation.
Language models can, to a certain extent, be automated or semi-automated, but the essence of their function, and their real power, is as a tool to augment human capabilities.
ey enable us to perform tasks more eciently, more accurately, and with greater clarity.
But they need strong supervision.
e notion that they could entirely replace human input was something we strongly disagreed with from the outset.
So, our philosophy was very much rooted in being able to help the teams use language models in the innovation process.
Yet, throughout the whole project, there was this tension.
On one side, there were voices advocating for full automation, and on the other, our steadfast belief in helping people use these tools, not replacing them with these tools.
By the end of the project, I think they completely agreed, and theyve publicly acknowledged this saying that language models radically improve how people do their jobs.
ey also publicly stated that while AI is vital, focusing solely on technology and overlooking investment in people is a signicant error.
To foster innovation and growth, companies need to invest equally in humans and AI.

Phase 3: Experimentation and Trial Q DAVID R : is phase involved small-scale experimentation to understand the AIs S capabilities and how it ts into existing workows.

322 f e traditional innovation approach is a lengthy and comprehensive process.
g is becomes more complex in a global company.
It involves consumer research, h various stages of development, and then distilling that research through a i series of workshops.
is process takes on many forms and templates, with key p milestones and meetings along the way.
Essentially, its a process devoid of AI, q relying instead on market research, brainstorming innovation trends, and a great r deal of creative writing based on these elements.
s What we ended up doing was collaborating with real teams worldwide, who t were accustomed to the old way, and assisting them in transitioning to the new, u AI-supported way.
In the process, we were dening what this new way entailed.
v In our exploration, we conducted dozens of case study exercises, developed in w partnership with the business.
We discovered that out of a total of 150 prompts x we developed, around 15 stood out as particularly useful.
However, these prompts y require personalization to the specic project, meaning you cant just apply them blindly you still have to engage your intellect.
One thing weve done with the prompts is to be explicit about what we want and the format we want it in.
e clearer you are in the prompt about your requirements, the clearer the responses.
is approach can lead to immediate product concepts.
I believe its incredibly crucial that the human working with AI gets to view the prompt.
is is the challenge I have with custom-built interfaces I cant view the prompts.
If I read someones custom-generated meeting summarization but cant view the prompt, I cant easily work out what it is doing (and not doing).
Is it seeking insights or nuggets or catchy phrases or bold phrases or timid phrases or action phrases?
Is it seeking specic actions or just insights?
ese are all aspects that I might care about or want to modify.
I genuinely believe concealing prompts is an error.
We tested whether we could go straight to creating a solution or inventing a product in a single step and without feeding any background knowledge into the language model.
e answer is that its possible, but the results are far from 323 excellent, and a long way short of outstanding.
e idea of automating the process is still elusive.
Jumping to a new product innovation in a single prompt does get you lots of thought starters, but this way its challenging to pinpoint where the responses go wrong and how to x them.
Essentially, our conclusion is that for any reasonably sophisticated requirement, you need to follow some kind of multi-step process when using AI.
You cant just leap to the answer.
You dont have to go through the entire process, but you should certainly follow some kind of procedure rather than jump straight to an answer in a single prompt.
ats a signicant lesson.
Phase 4: Realizing Practical Applications DAVID : Phase 4 involved enhancing the innovation process to realize tangible outcomes and benets.

e steps were meticulously planned and executed, keeping in mind the ultimate goal of enhancing the companys innovation capabilities and success rate.

We believe these skills are exactly what people need to harness the full potential of LLM technology.
e core of enhancing human capability lies in acquiring foundational skills.
Once these skills are acquired, the particular tools employed whether its Otter.
ai for summarization or Copilot for coding become less important, as these tools are bound to change over time.
However, the key is to understand their underlying operations.
Developing the ability to cra eective prompts, modify them, iterate, and manage data inputs is essential.
With these skills, you can adeptly navigate any large language model.
Essentially, our focus is on imparting these fundamental competencies.

Step a.
Clarity of desired outcome DAVID : e rst step is being very clear about what it is youre actually trying to achieve asking, What are my goals for this new product innovation?

Applying the logic Einstein suggested about solving a problem, if I had an hour to solve a problem, Id spend 55 minutes working out what the problem was and 5 minutes solving it.
It is the same with language models.
So, get to work on what problem youre trying to solve rst of all, before even starting on solving the problem.
325 Step b.
Understanding consumer needs DAVID : e second step focuses on deeply understanding consumer needs eectively and then understanding what consumer segments have those needs.
en, what unmet needs may be in the category?

Processing input materials that you already possess with AI is still a win.
You can process a signicantly larger number of documents and synthesize them more rapidly for the context of the product and the category youre working on.
If you lack that consumer research, thats not a problem.
You can extract useful consumer insights from the AIs training data, given that it has read a vast number of human reviews of products and services and possesses a deep understanding of underlying human needs.
erefore, theres another section of the client manual we produced that says, Dont have any inputs?

Read this chapter instead.
And it provides the types of prompts you can use to get the AI to provide you with useful human insights, audience segments, and so on.

is brings us on to the second LLM skill area of desktop research.

Certainly, I never saw any evidence that RAG was benecial.
We never depended on it in any of the processes we wrote.

r Key Learnings s t u Maintain focus with a roadmap v : Its crucial to have a clear vision of what youre w trying to achieve with a well-dened AI roadmap and be able to articulate it x to the right people.

342 e goal isnt only nancial quick wins, but to understand the drivers of the business and use this to improve performance and decision-making.

is involves lining up the systems for data capture and manipulation, and the tool to surface it more productively.
Be patient : Understand that building a robust, timely, monthly dataset is an iterative process.
You need to have patience as you build up a credible history.

M M INIMIZING CU s S TOMER CHURN WITH AI AI 349 Phase 1: Discovery and building the team MANU : I began by asking, Whats the biggest business problem you have?
e resounding response was customer attrition.
Acquiring new customers to replace your old ones is very challenging, so this gave me something meaningful to focus on.
e rst thing I did was go on a discovery mission to gure out how the business was currently addressing this problem.
Rather than reinventing the wheel, this meant I could identify a starting point from which we could build.
It turned out that the actuarial team had a basic statistical solution in place as part of a wider actuarial soware suite that created their prediction tables and pricing structures as well as tracking money in from premiums and out for claims.
is was over a decade old and suered from the impact of model and data dri.
In addition, it depended on a dated, small data statistical approach that could not leverage the rich signals and datasets available to the company.
e key problem with this solution was that it had low overall accuracy due to two things.
Firstly, the model was designed around a narrow set of inputs, using only a few predictor variables (age, gender, postcode, product type, claims, and premiums).
It lacked the sophistication to leverage the insurers richer customer and claims dataset.
Secondly, the simplistic modeling approach of the legacy solution meant it fell short of the business needs.
e statistical, multivariate regression model it used didnt consider the valuable time element of the customers journey.
Some regression-based approaches can be useful, but in this instance, it was designed more as a simple risk modeling tool and not a purpose-built churn prediction tool that could provide predictive insights into specic time horizons.
So, the business couldnt use it to proactively mitigate churn risk.

X Phase 2: Developing a minimum viable product (MVP) MANU : We focused on developing a data-driven mechanism to predict churn and address key shortcomings of the existing model.
I took in all the underlying datasets and created a new AI-driven system.
is system used Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, which are precursors to ChatGPT.
LSTM networks are widely used in deep learning and excel at capturing long-term dependencies, making them ideal for sequence prediction tasks.
ey can understand and predict patterns in sequential data like time series, text, and speech.
We outlined a 12-week approach to build and deliver an MVP (minimum viable product) that would demonstrate worth, gain traction, and start to earn trust.

t u v Explaining prediction w : People tend to think of prediction systems in binary x terms, as simply being accurate or not.
We spent considerable time explaining y the confusion matrix in detail false positives, false negatives, and so on and what the value of reducing false negatives would be for the business.
We used the analogy of COVID testing (topical at that time) and virus spread to explain the importance of precision to avoid false negatives.
A false negative in COVID testing meant you didnt pick someone who had the virus, and they went around spreading it, whereas a false positive might mean a person just got one more booster.
With this explainer, people really got the whole concept of false negative and positive scores, and how the former was potentially more serious.
Audience-based access : Not all information can be shared with everyone, so our approach to accessing data is to grant it based on privilege levels.
For instance, regulators and actuaries have access to more comprehensive information due to the nature of their roles.
is ensures that sensitive data is only accessible to those who need it for their specic functions, thereby maintaining data security and privacy.

s e prompt here is crucial you have to be specic.
Writing Summarize this s article is an absolutely terrible prompt.
So, I would say, Im interested in s marketing a tour by this person.
Im looking to understand the deep underlying s needs of his audiences.
Use this article to come up with a list of as many deep s and underlying needs that drive people to want to go see this artist as possible.
s We learned that in some instances, the more context you paste in, the less the s model pays attention to that context.

What magazines do they v read?
