is is crucial because you are not v marketing to the need; youre marketing to the broader person in the context of v 374 v their life so their related needs are really important to develop segments.
We go w from needs to audiences, which, in turn, give us audience segments, in a further w two or three prompts.
w I queried ChatGPT, You deeply understand audience motivations and behaviors.
w You have high business acumen, complex problem-solving skills, audience w empathy, and creative writing skills.
en I asked it to perform as: u v w An analyst at a top-tier audience strategy consulting rm x y A strategic and business-focused person An ethnographer A cooperative researcher A creative writer y We used several series of prompts to expand and dig deeper, as described above, y on each driver or facet of fame and develop proles outlining the typical lifestyle y attitudes, behaviors, and interests of likely fans who would be drawn by that y specic aspect of the artists oering.
is helped us boil down the original 15 y to a list of 7-8 core targetable audience clusters, which is a manageable number y to debate.
None of these were characterized by age or music genre taste, but y by deep underlying needs, which is a totally dierent way of thinking about y marketing the event.
y Previously, we would go through this laborious process ourselves, reading y extensively and conducting interviews with a wide variety of people in the industry.
It could take weeks to compile a useful list of dimensions for a topic or theme.
In this instance, we did it in hours.
In the end, every one of these segments would be valid candidates to go aer and position the event and marketing around, but you cant do them all.
So, you probably have to pick one, two, or three and then go deep and market to them.
At the end of the day, this takes an awful lot of judgment.
Because theyre all valid ways to describe people, you cant really be wrong about these segments.
e kinds of tests you can therefore apply are, for example, asking, Is this segment 375 useful?
Does it t the creative vision of the person?
Is it actionable?
Ultimately, its about instinct and this is one of the reasons that you cant automate this stu, because I cant articulate all the criteria Im judging it on its a task that lives outside of ChatGPT.
Phase 3: Developing a tailored positioning and strategy DAVID : Finally, we leveraged ChatGPT to develop needs, pain points, and ideas aligned to the values of each group, helping us begin to tailor marketing approaches to the values and preferences of each fan group.
Asking ChatGPT to work out what creative writing, positioning on adverts and marketing, as well as the imagery that would engage and excite each group.
roughout this process, we continuously applied human expertise by iteratively rening the prompts and thoughtfully curating the most viable outputs.
is infusion of real-world business judgment prevented impractical suggestions or those with a narrow focus from passing into the nal strategy recommendations.
It was about combining my expertise, because I know what a good audience segmentation and strategy looks like, and that of the promoter, who knows the artist and their goals.
I couldnt have done it without the promoter, and they couldnt have done it without me and neither of us could have done it without ChatGPT.
To take a more detailed view, this is what the prompt process looks like: Because ChatGPT works best with relatively small specic tasks, I asked, for each of the six segments, Please write three bullet points on how we might reduce their pain points as it relates to a night out like this.
en I asked for three innovations we could do to make the event experience uniquely meet the needs of this audience.
en, for each segment, I asked ChatGPT to think through three positioning strap lines and ways to position the event that might uniquely meet the needs of this segment.
I use a lot of prompts.
is is the kind of thing we would chain and do automatically because were just thinking through stu.
Were not going to use all of it; its more like brainstorming.
376 e nal result is a creative brief with key messages, tone of voice, and creative direction that would engage each segment.
D Results and Impact DAVID : e approach resulted in increased revenue (ticket sales), cost savings, and time to insight to drive the strategy.
e nal cost of our innovative method was less than 5% of the usual expense for an audience strategy project, delivered in 20 hours instead of 5-6 weeks.
is demonstrates the potential of large language models like ChatGPT in signicantly reducing both time and cost in strategic planning, resulting in a more impactful event with a higher return on investment (in ticket sales).
Its very rare for a promoter like this to have a metric they want to move before doing the work.
Here, the key metric was sales.
e challenge is that theres no counterfactual to this specically: how much would you have sold if we didnt do the marketing in this way?
ere was no A/B testing of marketing strategies.

Key Learnings Perspective broadening : Beyond augmenting skills and saving time, LLMs are important tools to bring in broader perspectives for business idea generation.
ey can generate a higher volume of ideas, but with a greater variance of quality.
erefore, this necessitates the human expertise to discern the most promising concepts.
Importantly, LLMs dont replace human creativity in the crucial task of combining these ideas and parameters into innovative, high-quality solutions.
LLM prompt optimization : LLMs like ChatGPT are augmenting business processes by speeding up operations, providing valuable insights, and aiding strategic decision-making.
However, ChatGPT requires specic detailed prompts to achieve optimal results.
Furthermore, the process involves multiple steps and iterative renement with expert curation at each stage to ensure high-quality outputs and prevent error propagation.
LLMs democratize data and analytics : LLMs provide smaller businesses access to data and analytics tools that were previously exclusive to larger corporations.
LLMs provide an aordable alternative to traditional market research and audience segmentation, leveling the playing eld and facilitating growth and innovation across all business sizes.
Human-AI collaboration : While automation can produce satisfactory results, excellence requires human decision-making.
e eective use of AI demands human involvement at each stage of results, to optimize quality and prevent error propagation.
is expertise and judgment cannot be automated.

A low-code or no-code approach is oen used to create AI solutions more eciently, employing a user-friendly drag-and-drop interface and pre-built templates.
is system automatically applies data quality and master data service rules.
is approach assumes the infrastructure is cloud-based with governance built in, so solutions can scale up or down as needed, making value realization agile and ecient.
us, compliance happens by design rather than enforcement.

ey allow the data and AI team to work in their preferred manner with a governed framework for tracking and measuring results.
is environment can be easily automated using tools like YAML and DevOps pipelines, eliminating the need for manual intervention.

Testing throughout the entire lifecycle To ensure successful and optimized AI implementation, prioritize rigorous testing throughout the entire lifecycle.
is includes integrating human expertise (HITL) for quality assurance, especially during training data creation.
A dedicated DataOps team streamlines testing and ensures high-quality data throughout the AI development process.
To catch issues early and optimize performance before public release, leverage internal super users through a rigorous internal testing process (dogfooding).

3 Organizing talent 1 Many organizations begin with a distributed model for AI skills, which oers 1 speed, exibility, and the ability to tailor services and solutions.
However, this 2 approach can lead to several drawbacks, including varying maturity levels, 2 redundant eorts and investments, missed opportunities for the cross-fertilization 2 of practices, and higher overall costs.
2 At the other extreme, a fully centralized model oers economies of scale, common 2 governance and risk management, and shared resources but can be slow to 2 respond to specic business needs.
2 e proposed hub-and-spoke model provides a more ecient and adaptable 2 approach, aligning well with evolving business models.
It leverages the benets 2 of central oversight, governance, and risk management while enabling the 2 consolidation of investments and promoting a degree of autonomy and agility 3 within the business.
is model features a lean Center of Excellence (COE) with 3 expertise in data science, data engineering, operations, governance, and data 3 leadership.
e COE sets standards for solution development, ensures repeatability, 3 and promotes advanced capabilities.
Hands-on AI teams, oen including data 3 scientists and analysts, are integrated into the business, to ensure agility and 3 alignment with specic needs.

47 ADOPTION 48 AI Adoption and Managing Change 49 What strategies and processes does your organization have in place 4@ to manage the transition toward AI adoption?
Are your employees 4A equipped and ready?
D In a recent D meeting with a senior healthcare leader, I was asked to discuss a failure D in an AI project.
e example that came immediately to mind was a failure in D adoption and change management in a company where it was assumed everyone D was onboard with the objective to become an AI-led organization and committed D to the work needed to get there.
However, the reality proved otherwise.
is D failure in adoption and change management is a common issue, echoed repeatedly D throughout this book and by other practitioners.
Successful AI implementation E has both a technical aspect and a human element.
e involvement of people E within the organization as a linchpin of successful AI adoption is repeatedly E evidenced in our interviews.
E A E Harvard Business Review E article by Randy Bean ( E packt.
link/hbr E ) revealed the E struggle of many Fortune 1000 companies.
Bean highlighted that the barrier to E becoming data-driven is not just legacy tech but embedded cultures that are resistant F to new ways of doing things.
Interviews and case studies in this book support F this notion, revealing a stronger resistance in longer-established organizations.
F To overcome this challenge, Bean suggests a solution lies in engaging the people F who will work with and be aected by AI in every step of the process.
W W HERE W W EVE G G OT T T O AND W W HAT s S N N EXT 422 F Focusing on the people and processes oen yields quicker and more successful F results.
Conversely, projects that lead with technology tend to be slower and have F a lower success rate.
Driving adoption and ownership of the solution or model F within the organization is crucial its not a case of build it and hope theyll use F it.
Users need to have a vested interest in the creation of these solutions because F having skin in the game helps them realize its value.
G e successful application of AI requires clear executive directives in a top-down G approach and a comprehensive change management program.
Active participation G from every level of the organization in a bottom-up approach is also essential.
G Understanding how to implement these changes in the details of daily work is G also needed.
Questions such as Why is this important to my job?
, What do G I need to do dierently?
, and Can I trust it?
should be addressed.
Its crucial G to ensure a team understands the benets of the new tools or solutions, how to G use them, and the improvements they can bring to their work.
Engaging with G the people who will ask these questions is critical, making them active agents of G change rather than passive recipients.
H To overcome H resistance, frame the project as an experiment rather than a xed H end goal and understand the limitations of your AI solutions.
Respect the fact H that the learning process is continuous and accept it includes failures that lead H to incremental successes.
H e business must own the solution, with a clear and concise use case that allows H for incremental improvements and measurable progress.
If successful, theres a H virtuous cycle as the business starts saving money, stakeholders become satised, H and they begin to use more AI solutions and capabilities.
H Adoption and change begin with early initiatives aimed at achieving quick wins I in areas where the value of the AI solution can be immediately demonstrated.
I Once these initial successes are achieved, the changes can be scaled throughout I the rest of the organization.
Success oen starts with enhancing eciencies I in operational areas like nance, HR, and customer service.
Learnings are I subsequently extended to market products and services.
is approach ensures I a systematic and eective adoption of change.
Success in one area oen sparks I change in others.

W W HERE W W EVE G G OT T T O AND W W HAT s S N N EXT 424 5H In closing T is book has explored the current AI landscape, driven by a strong interest in U the potential of AI to propel sustained productivity growth across industries.
We U opened with the thesis that sustained productivity growth is, in part, contingent U on the eective integration and board adoption of AI solutions across a range U of sectors.
U Tech leaders such as Amazon, Google, Netix, Meta, Microso, and OpenAI have U been investing in AI for over a decade, and their products have become integral U to our daily lives.
However, AI hasnt yet been put to work by wider industries U and translated into ecient growth.
U We are still in the early stages of implementing AI in business operations.
Large U enterprises with established partnerships with the tech leaders, gaining access to V cutting-edge AI tools and expertise, have a clear advantage.
For some businesses, V particularly newer companies built on cloud-based technology, AI adoption V comes naturally.
However, for others, creating value and gaining a competitive V edge through AI might be more challenging.
Technology and investment alone V arent enough to drive V transformation.
We are likely to see a signicant impact V over the next 5 to 10 years.
erefore, the crucial question remains: how can V organizations best integrate AI to achieve signicant productivity gains?
V AI is a rapidly evolving domain.
Its transformative potential in business, the need for V its ethical application, and the innovative leadership required all present signicant W challenges for organizations.
ese challenges are particularly pronounced when W deciding how best to invest in AI technologies.
Yet, as weve seen throughout W the interviews, technology innovation and monetary investment alone are not W sucient to drive transformation.
Integrating AI requires a long-term perspective.
W It takes sustained eort and ongoing iterations.
Failures are inevitable, yet they W can pave the way for incremental successes and continuous improvement.

u 395 p About the Author Lisa Weaver-Lambert has been leveraging data to solve complex commercial challenges for over two decades through her roles at Microso, Accenture, and in capital and public markets.
In addition, she has held executive line management positions and served at board level.
Her work in regions including Europe, the US, APAC, and Africa has brought her a global perspective.
Lisas early career in nancial services, working on acquisition, retention, and fraud detection programs, provided a valuable foundation in data-driven decision making.
is experience solidied her understanding of data quality, governance, and its commercial potential.
She witnessed rsthand the power of large datasets and how working with data could solve real-world business problems.
Since then, she has worked with some of the worlds best-known brands towards success, earning her recognition as a leading woman in technology in the EMEA investment eld.
Her extensive experience in strategy and business transformation has led her to collaborate with organizations of dierent sizes and maturity levels to accelerate performance.
Recognizing the transformative potential of AI, Lisas mission is to equip non-technical leaders with the knowledge and condence to leverage AI for their organizations.
Lisa recently founded Oxford AI Studio.
is initiative aims at bridging industry with researchers and knowledge institutes to tackle real-world business challenges and drive the development and application of responsible AI innovation.
Lisa earned her degrees from the University of Lancaster and ESCP Business School in Paris, strengthening her qualications through executive programs at MIT Sloan School of Management and UC Berkeley School of Information.
LinkedIn: lisa-weaver-lambert SARVARTH MI s S RA 112 y LISA : Can you give a brief overview of the services you provide?
SARVARTH : ContractPodAi oers a suite of features including Leah, a generative AI-powered legal assistant, and an intuitive Contract Lifecycle Management (CLM) platform for end-to-end contract management.
It also provides robust analytics to optimize operations, a legal intake solution for request organization, and translation capabilities for over 60 languages.
e company operates on a Soware-as-a-Service (SaaS) model, oering continuous updates and support to its subscribers.
LISA : Can you describe your companys AI-led growth and business model distinction?
SARVARTH : As experienced UK commercial lawyers, this is where we started.
Initially, we thought our market would be SMEs (subject-matter experts) or mid-cap businesses, as we assumed larger organizations would already have contract management systems in place.
However, to our surprise, when we got the product to market, we started gaining traction with very large organizations like Bosch Siemens.
Its the usual entrepreneurial journey you think you know your market audience, but you end up somewhere else.
e pain points were quite similar across the board there was no single source of truth for contracts, the process was broken, and it wasnt done well.
Innovative GCs who were more tech-savvy than their predecessors found this frustrating and were eager for change.
We began attracting these large companies with similar pain points, and thus, the product-market t started veering into the late mid-market to enterprise space.
Our rst customers were eectively large businesses, which was quite accidental.
Fast forward to 2019, we established a presence in the US, specically New York.
Over the next 12 months, we saw similar acceptance of the product-market t.
Weve taken a fairly unique journey in the enterprise B2B soware space.
is business hadnt taken any institutional money and was fairly bootstrapped.
It had a reasonably good ARR (Annual Recurring Revenue) great retention SARVARTH MI s S RA 113 numbers and returning customers.
is is quite dierent from most of our peers who probably spent $20 million building a Minimum Viable Product (MVP).
Our companys growth was initially surprising to analysts due to our tangible revenue base with minimal funding.
Despite the lack of a CTO and being founded by two lawyers, we managed to grow our product platform.
We then partnered with global venture capital and private equity fund, Insight Partners to transition from a bootstrap business to a scale-up, focusing on growth with a scientic approach.
We started building an operating model, considering sales, demand generation, enablement, and building a marketing organization from scratch.
From late 2019 to 2021, we maintained a signicant growth rate and market traction.
en, in September 2021, we secured a substantial funding round ofover $100 millionfromSoBank.
is signicant investment was strategically aimed at shaping the companys trajectory over the next ve years and how en-US we would devise a strategy from both a product and go-to-market motion to lead the legal technology market in our areas of strength.
is funding raise allowed us to continue growing and innovating on the product side so we could serve our customer base with truly cutting-edge technology.
Our focus has been on enterprise customers, positioning ourselves as a legal lead contract lifecycle management player.
We empower legal operators and counsel as decision-makers in buying enterprise technology that benets them.
Weve been able to maintain a good balance sheet.
We have always been committed to growth thats underpinned by scientic principles, and this has consistently been our core focus.
Weve always wanted to make sure we become a little bit more ecient, and thats whats helped us along the way.
Weve always thought about sales, demand generation, enablement, and building a marketing organization from the ground up.
